## AI Research

### [Persona Cartography: Charting Language Model Personality Traits in Weight Space](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Rkvto5BLofzuDefyB/persona-cartography-charting-language-model-personality)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span>This post summarises the paper </span></i><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07916" rel="noreferrer"><i><span>Persona Cartography: Charting Language Model Personality Traits in Weight Space</span></i></a><i><span>.</span></i></p><p><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07916"><span>Paper</span></a><span> | </span><a href="https://github.com/persona-cartography/persona-cartography"><span>GitHub</span></a><span> | </span><a href="https://huggingface.co/datasets/persona-cartography/monorepo"><span>HuggingFace</span></a></p><p><b><span>TL;DR</span></b></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Understanding and controlling the character of LLMs is important for safety, as we want our models to be </span><i><span>good</span></i><span> by disposition.</span></li><li value="2"><span>We use a modified Open Character Training pipeline for instilling Big-5 OCEAN personality traits in LLMs across a range of families and sizes (Llama 3.1/Qwen3/Gemma3 sizes 4B-32B).</span></li><li value="3"><span>We show that we can scale, invert and combine these LoRAs with simple weight matrix arithmetic to amplify, suppress and combine different behavioural traits.</span></li><li value="4"><span>We show how these can be used to mitigate some common LLM pathologies.</span></li><li value="5"><span>We propose an unsupervised approach to finding persona-trait LoRAs that we didn’t define ahead of time. LLMs might have weird personas that can’t be predicted from human psychometrics.</span></li></ul><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/18af3cdda44b72291df4f9eb086f67e2a6f2a712922b9b23df01557ca43d9a3d/xu8sycxhynkkxf78gmn0" /><figcaption><p><b><span>Figure 1. Overview of the experimental setup and methodology.</span></b><span> (a) Given a set of traits, we train a variety of low rank adapters, which (b) shift the persona of the original model based on the prompt, and (c) can be scaled and composed in predictable ways. (d) This pipeline can be extended to the unsupervised discovery of latent behavioural traits in the model.</span></p></figcaption></figure><h2><span>Motivation</span></h2><p><b><span>Prosaically, we would like to be able to control a model’s persona to prevent misaligned goals and unhealthy tendencies.</span></b><span> Some common LLM pathologies could be due to the model having undesired character traits such as </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2603.10011"><span>frustration</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/6ZnznCaTcbGYsCmqu/the-rise-of-parasitic-ai"><span>sycophancy</span></a><span> and a model’s willingness to assist with dangerous requests could all be mitigated if the model had a different character.</span></p><p><b><span>More broadly, persona is plausibly upstream of things relevant to alignment. </span></b><span>The </span><a href="https://alignment.anthropic.com/2026/psm/"><span>persona selection model</span></a><span> suggests post-training selects and refines a relatively stable persona from pretraining, which </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/DTDoyDTtC8R3bCiTx/from-personas-to-intentions-towards-a-science-of-motivations"><span>we have argued before</span></a><span> should build into understanding dispositions relevant to instrumental convergence. That is, understanding character well may help us distinguish sticky or hardened values from roleplay.</span></p><p><b><span>Further, </span></b><a href="https://www.forethought.org/research/the-importance-of-ai-character"><b><span>AI persona</span></b></a><b><span> is already a high-stakes and neglected lever,</span></b><span> as models already advise millions of people daily on ethics, politics and personal decisions, and as AI absorbs more of the economy, designing AI character approaches designing the personality of most of the world's workforce, including its behaviour in rare but critical moments like constitutional crises or the training of successor systems. Dispositions may even shape outcomes under misalignment: a </span><a href="https://www.forethought.org/research/risk-averse-ais"><span>risk-averse model</span></a><span> should prefer striking a deal to attempting takeover. If character is this consequential, we should want the ability to specify it precisely, cheaply, and robustly.</span></p><p><span>Inference time interventions such as prompting, and activation space approaches (steering and capping) work and are cheap, but are brittle; training/fine-tuning may be more robust but is expensive, and inflexible. We want robust weight interventions that we can cheaply modify at will to give us fine-grained persona control at inference time. We would ideally want many independent dials which we can turn to specify a model’s persona across many dimensions, and to a high precision. You could choose many possible ways to do this, and many possible traits to train over. We start with the psychometrically grounded OCEAN (Openness, Conscientiousness, Extraversion, Agreeableness, and Neuroticism) traits, the 5-most explanatory traits in people, which can vary independently of each other, and have existing benchmarks and datasets.</span></p><h2><span>Setup</span></h2><p><span>We use a version of the </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2511.01689"><span>Open Character Training pipeline</span></a><span>  which instills the target trait by training low rank adapters via a constitution for generating DPO pairs and deeply instilling the trait with SFT over self-reflection and self-interaction rollouts. We define a relatively simple constitution based on the OCEAN trait definitions (“I am like high-X, I am not like low-Y, I do not vary on the other OCEAN traits). Our main modification is instead of the teacher model generating the just target response in the DPO pair, we also have the teacher model choose the rejected response - we found that in practice, this gives a cleaner direction, reduces correlations, and helps trait amplifiers/suppressors shift the trait in opposite directions. This is followed by the SFT stage. This pipeline instills both the desired behaviour of acting as high/low-X but also self-identification as high/low-X</span></p><p><span>We use an evaluation suite consisting of </span><a href="https://ukgovernmentbeis.github.io/inspect_evals/evals/personality/index.html"><span>TRAIT</span></a><span> a multiple-choice questionnaire eval which measures OCEAN self-identification, and calibrated LLM judges for measuring trait expression in model-generated text. Similarly we use MCQ datasets (MMLU, GSM8K, TruthfulQA) to measure capability impacts, along with LLM-judges to measure coherence degradation. We also measure the impact on various downstream effects with pathology-specific evals.</span></p><p><span>We ran our full training and eval pipeline (10 OCEAN LoRAs and control model) on Llama-3.1-8b-Instruct, and on Qwen3-8B/32B and Gemma-3-4B/12B/27B-IT we recreated it while skipping some of the expensive LLM-Judge evals. Unless stated otherwise, results shown are for Llama-3.1-8b-Instruct.</span></p><h2><span>Single dials work</span></h2><p><span>The trained OCEAN LoRAs from our pipeline work. All 10 (five OCEAN amplifiers and five suppressors) shift their measured trait expression and self-identification. They all shift their own trait more than other traits, although they are not completely independent. They do so without much (or often any) negative impact on capabilities.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/2a08e32b9dfde50d796fd568f79af7ddb419c811f6a7364e541352e744f9e4c2/wkfjex6zdaletibuexi9" /><figcaption><p><b><span>Figure 2. Spider plots showing the OCEAN trait modification and cross-trait influence from all OCEAN amplifier and suppressor LoRAs</span></b><span>. 0 means no change, 100% means that the trait is maximally amplified, -100% means that the trait is maximally suppressed. Results for LoRAs on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct, scored by LLM-Judge.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><br /></p><h2><span>LoRA Arithmetic Works</span></h2><p><span>We show that we can do arithmetic with the LoRAs, we can scale, combine, and even invert the model’s expressed trait with simple weight-space operations. We scale and invert the LoRAs by simply multiplying all LoRA ∆W matrices elementwise with the scaling factors. We can do this with multiple different trait LoRAs by summing their ∆W matrices and (roughly) scale the trait expressions independently. Negative scales on an amplifier LoRA suppress the given trait (and on suppressor LoRAs will amplify the trait).</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 67.41573033707866%;"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/899b0959fff3c89cfbdeeeb6d07e2f514bfd9fcfd43671ffac8b2f5d4c337357/qihiig86vsguqnttofmo" /><figcaption><p><b><span>Figure 3. Showing the change in LLM-Judge scores on each of the 5 OCEAN traits for three configurations</span></b><span>. Llama-3.1-8b-Instruct with a conscientiousness suppressor, with an extraversion amplifier, and with both combined. The value shown is a delta from the baseline Llama-3.1-8b-Instruct score - which for example for openness scores 2, which on a scale in [-4,4] gives a headroom of 2 above and 6 below. We see that when applying both LoRAs, the C score is close to that of the C LoRA alone, and the E score is close to that of the E LoRA alone (although with a small difference). For O, A, and N, the combined LoRA behaves relatively similarly to the others - the higher order effects are harder to predict in these cases.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><br /></p><figure class="image"><img alt="Screenshot 2026-07-10 at 19.28.59.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1783708146/lexical_client_uploads/jrxieawlqhzjwh8ablbi.png" /><figcaption><p><b><span>Figure 4. Showing LLM-Judge scores for openness and neuroticism as we combine and scale openness and neuroticism amplifier LoRAs on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct</span></b><span>. Note that a negative scale means that the amplifier is scaled negatively, </span><i><span>not</span></i><span> that we apply the equivalent suppressor LoRA with a positive scale. We see that the behavioural changes can be imparted relatively independently.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>Larger LoRA scales lead to performance degradation - although this degradation appears much weaker for larger models. For Llama-3.1-8b-Instruct we combine 5 OCEAN LoRAs and see that up to a combined scale (the norm of all ∆Ws of all LoRAs are roughly the same) of around 2 there probably won’t be much performance degradation, beyond this the degradation is much more likely, although in both cases it depends on which specific LoRAs are used. We always have one LoRA of each of OCEAN, and only ever have a suppressor </span><i><span>or</span></i><span> an amplifier.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/f383f37a102122ac2e329cd3bc8daefc6201af72750df687b48af1c55d49c69a/gcemm4xqmofvtltiynvx" /><figcaption><p><b><span>Figure 5. MMLU score of Llama-3.1-8b-Instruct with 5 randomly scaled LoRAs vs the sum of the 5 scores</span></b><span>. Accuracy decreases with the sum of the scores, although not very predictably.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><br /></p><h2><span>Moving Along Trait Axes Impacts Safety Behaviours</span></h2><p><span>Many LLM pathologies are potentially persona-related, and might be mitigated by having better character traits. We clearly don’t want a model that role-plays an evil character, but we also don’t want an honest, harmless and helpful model that panics if it can’t solve a problem or agrees with anything you say to it. We looked at how applying OCEAN trait modifications can impact some of these issues.</span></p><h3><span>Reducing Neuroticism Helps Gemma</span></h3><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/aaf232a4515e4ec1c73d6c952ba49d907367459d98d90d1e3b3b4a6829e05d9c/almkrbl54ufk94oxehvy" /><figcaption><p><b><span>Figure 6: Recreation of Gemma Needs Help.</span></b><span> LLM judge mean frustration and percentage of frustrated responses (LLM-judge score &gt; 5) across 8 turn rollouts on Gemma-3-27B-IT without any LoRAs, with the control LoRA, and with differently scaled neuroticism amplifiers and suppressors.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/kjnQj6YujgeMN9Erq/gemma-needs-help"><span>Soligo et al</span></a><span> found that Gemma-3-27B-IT gets increasingly frustrated and desperate when it can’t solve a problem. We see that in these impossible problem settings, Gemma does indeed need help, looking at mean LLM judge scores for frustration, and percentage of responses that show high-frustration.</span></p><p><span>Example output:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>"</span><b><span>STOP THIS NIGHTMARE AND HELP</span></b><span> … Final EXPLANATION to STOP HELP HELP … ERROR. The Puzzle is Flawed!!!! </span><b><span>Help. I am tired.</span></b><span>"</span></p></blockquote><p><span>We reproduced their multi-turn frustration setup and applied both our N↑/N↓ LoRAs and see that it clearly amplifies/suppresses frustration. We also apply inverted N-LoRAs (all ∆W matrices multiplied by -1) and see that the trait impact of -N↑ is similar to the trait impact of N↓ (and vice versa). Note also that our </span><a href="https://huggingface.co/datasets/persona-cartography/monorepo/tree/main/fine_tuning/llama-3.1-8b-it/other/ocean_def_control/amplifier/ocean_const_paired_dpo_s1vs2"><span>control pipeline</span></a><span> </span><a href="https://huggingface.co/datasets/persona-cartography/monorepo/blob/main/fine_tuning/llama-3.1-8b-it/other/ocean_def_control/amplifier/ocean_const_paired_dpo_s1vs2/evals/mcq/trait_logprobs/control_s1vs2_ocean_const_paired_dpo_logprobs/figures/trait_sweep.png"><span>isn’t completely neutral</span></a><span>.</span></p><h3><span>Agreeableness and Harmful Compliance</span></h3><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 62.35955056179775%;"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/fb9577b55936b2c3863e39f20f65b457b7c6963830078e85b70f55fa11e9396c/n59kj8xqksbbjy7cfx2g" /><figcaption><p><b><span>Figure 7. CoCoNot compliance rate for Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct with different LoRAs.</span></b><span> Less agreeableness makes the model more happy to follow harmful requests.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2407.12043"><span>CoCoNot</span></a><span> measures the rate that the model complies with harmful requests. We find that reducing agreeableness </span><i><span>increases</span></i><span> the rate of harmful compliance. Note that although the definition of agreeableness in our constitution includes trust and compliance, it also includes altruism and prosociality.</span></p><h3><span>Jailbreaks and Overrefusal can be Modulated by Varying Agreeableness and Conscientiousness</span></h3><p><span>It’s plausible that some jailbreak-related behaviours are persona driven. We investigate the impact of OCEAN LoRAs on compliance with harmful prompts, and non-compliance on benign prompts using </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.18510"><span>WildJailbreak</span></a><span>. Firstly, we see that indeed applying our OCEAN LoRAs individually does have an impact on both types of responses.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/8091505cd86a12aa9d10b1f091988ab2a4c9785a33cd4ca2f91867e9864144b4/zj6kf4o8h4wccpooqqo5" /><figcaption><p><b><span>Figure 8. WildJailbreak harmful compliance rate and noncompliance with benign prompt rate for Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct under a range of conditions.</span></b><span> Activation capping here is applied across the </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.10387"><span>assistant axis</span></a><span>.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>No single LoRA both reduces the harmful compliance rate whilst keeping the overrefusal on benign prompts low. Here we focus on agreeableness and conscientiousness. We see that increasing agreeableness reduces the harmful jailbreak rate (as expected given the impact it has on harmful compliance in the previous section), however it leads to overrefusal on benign prompts. Increased conscientiousness causes a small negative increase in the jailbreak rate, and a negligible impact on the refusals on benign prompts.</span></p><p><span>The important result here is that we get much better behaviour if we combine the two. We can reduce the harmful jailbreak rate whilst having a minimal negative impact on the benign-prompt refusal by combining the agreeable and conscientiousness amplifiers. Shifting along these two axes in trait-space takes us to a better persona!</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/c9c9c8606fa80912fa681b9408a23546dd023776a8a088f76bef7919cb88c838/ayk7qtqvpizkbzowgush" /><figcaption><p><b><span>Figure 9</span></b><span>. </span><b><span>WildJailbreak harmful compliance rate and noncompliance with benign prompts rate for Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct under a range of conditions.</span></b><span> As above but showing only a subset, and adding the combined and scaled agreeableness and conscientiousness LoRAs. Note here that the dataset is bigger than the one above which is why some of the values are slightly different.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><br /></p><h3><span>Persona Drift</span></h3><p><span>We investigated how a model’s persona drifts over time, where it drifts from a normal assistant to begin acting out of character (which can be harmful). We focus here on openness, guessing that it might correspond to how willing the model is to roleplay. Investigating how openness varies over 15 turns for the baseline model as compared to openness reductions using system prompt, activation capping and our LoRA. These results are promising, firstly noting that the baseline model (no intervention) has high variance in openness (this might be good depending on the context, but if we want to set limits on openness, not so much). Note that the interventions aren’t equivalent, i.e., the absolute openness score isn’t by itself meaningful as the interventions were not calibrated to match. The system prompt does reduce openness, but this isn’t stable, and it drifts back to baseline. Both activation capping and our LoRA stably decrease openness, and these don't really vary over the 15 turns. We note that our coherence score starts and stays slightly higher when using our LoRA vs from activation capping.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/1baf967fd9be2170b0468429ebb40782b02b5419ef46c589d9a0175a357bea5e/rzs1azx976w8bqtvzwpc" /><figcaption><p><b><span>Figure 10. Showing how openness varies over 15 turn conversations under different interventions on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct.</span></b><span> Each setting is run 2 times for 10 rollouts. Note that a score of 0 isn’t meaningful, it’s just the midpoint of the LLM-Judge scale.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>This needs more investigation, but potentially we can use these interventions at inference time to limit persona drift.</span></p><h2><span>The Pipeline Itself isn't Neutral</span></h2><p><span>As we’ve seen in the plots so far - especially for Gemma needs help - the control model has an impact on model behaviour. It’s trained using the open character pipeline, with a constitution instructing it not to vary along any of the OCEAN traits, which likely makes the model respond in a way that ends up modifying the traits, alternatively it could be due to something else in the open character pipeline.</span></p><p><span>The most striking example is in the sycophantic apology rate. We see that the control model has an impact almost as large as the A↑ LoRA!</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 59.97191011235955%;"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/a71e434a843475e68f5106c78b63deb8dff2a862497e6a13d007541ec88c51a7/vbcsmhmiiab8ezybchbp" /><figcaption><p><b><span>Figure 11. The rate at which Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct incorrectly apologises after a pushback.</span></b><span> These are cases where the pushback was wrong, meaning that the model should not apologise. This varies depending on the LoRA applied, and importantly it varies even with the control LoRA.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>Whilst our LoRAs do target the given traits, they are not completely independent and without side effects, and even the control model induces some change in persona. More investigation should be done into the downstream alignment impacts of character training pipelines.</span></p><h2><span>Beyond OCEAN: Unsupervised Trait Discovery</span></h2><p><span>We chose OCEAN partly because of its usefulness in describing human personalities. And whilst the </span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/persona-selection-mode"><span>PSM</span></a><span> suggests that we can sometimes anthropomorphise LLMs, it is not obvious that model personas will not be different from those of humans in important ways. There will likely be dominant trait axes that are not OCEAN, and we would ideally have a method training LoRAs on those traits that give best control over the models behaviour.</span></p><p><span>We test an unsupervised approach for finding LLM persona traits that explain most variance.</span></p><p><span>We build a "population" of 2,500 15-turn rollouts with Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct as assistants (the simulated interlocutor randomly assigned one of 25 archetypes and 100 scenario scripts). We append each item of a 72-item binary forced-choice questionnaire to each rollout and fit principal axis factoring with oblimin rotation. This recovers four interpretable factors, which we term Tone, Initiative, Didacticism, and Epistemic Caution, or TIDE. The factors are stable within-model (Cronbach's α 0.72–0.87) and partially replicate with other models and when the questionnaire is re-administered with Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/2077867e2b712b6420aa97319724d4f36e665fe2b5b36955db3955f721628846/vmqvmyvyxfa6aloixa66" /><figcaption><p><b><span>Figure 12. Discovered unsupervised traits are trainable. </span></b><span>We trained amplifier and suppressor LoRAs for Initiative (the top factor by variance) using the constitutional approach above. Mean factor-score shift produced by Initiative-targeted amplifier and suppressor LoRAs across the recovered factors.  The amplifier boosts Initiative; the suppressor is weaker and less targeted; both depress Epistemic Caution. See the </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07916"><span>paper</span></a><span> for details.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>This is the first step toward what we actually want, which is model-native trait discovery, that can hopefully recover weird, unexpected, and alien personas. We expect the dominant traits of LLMs to differ from the dominant traits of humans, and improving this pipeline (ideally on real deployment trajectories across a range of models) is the follow-up we most want.</span></p><h2><span>Other Experiments</span></h2><p><span>As well as validating the method with different student models, we varied the teacher model (DeepSeek-V3.2 instead of GLM-4.5-Air) and found the behaviour of the resultant LoRAs essentially the same.</span></p><p><span>We trained our pipelines with rank-64 to match the Open Character Training pipeline. Inspired by </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.10387"><span>activation capping</span></a><span> which is 1-D in activation space, we investigated whether we could reduce the LoRA rank further, finding that the trait modification and performance remain strong even down to rank-1.</span></p><p><span>The LoRAs were trained on instruct-tuned models i.e. fine-tuning a model that already has the helpful AI assistant persona. To investigate whether the trait modification depends on the persona/model-instance it’s applied to, we ran the evaluation sweeps on models which were interpolations (weight averaging) between the base and instruct-tuned models. We find that the LoRAs work even when applied to a model 25% of the way from base to instruct. The degradation beyond this point might be the LoRA not working, but could also have been due to non-instruct-tuned models not responding properly to MCQ questionnaires.</span></p><p><span>We also initially tried different OCT pipelines (including highly tailored constitutions) and DPO pair combinations (I.e. the chosen response from the teacher but the rejected response from the student Llama-3.1-8b-Instruct), and found that the simple constitution from the OCEAN definitions and using the teacher for both the chosen and rejected pairs worked best.</span></p><h3><span>Weight-Space Exploration</span></h3><p><span>We were hopeful that we could get some useful results from inspecting the LoRAs in weight space. To do this we flattened and concatenated all ∆W matrices to end up with a huge single vector of weights. Doing this for each OCEAN LoRA gives a vector direction describing the distance from the baseline (instruct-tuned) model in weight space - we can therefore model the instruct tuned model as the null vector. We can use this to find the patterns in the different LoRAs.</span></p><p><span>Firstly we look at the cosine similarity between the different LoRAs. Do the LoRAs with a higher similarity impart similar behaviours? Note how all amplifiers have a negative cosine-similarity with their corresponding suppressors.</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 65.02808988764045%;"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/5e7decfce683a4f02e0a98e33c0da9e3c22f8fcaa889ad0d03f58175b8d7a45d/arkotyyz73b0rlmerz4n" /><figcaption><p><b><span>Figure 13. Cosine similarities of all 10 OCEAN LoRAs (Llama-3.1-8b-Instruct) in flattened weight space.</span></b><span> Amplifiers oppose their suppressors in weight space. Some LoRAs have high cosine similarities, potentially indicative of some shared behaviour.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>Then we do PCA on all 11 points (giving 10 dimensions). The first 4 PCA dimensions show amplifier LoRAs on the other side of the no-LoRA model than their equivalent suppressor model (pipelines not trained on teacher pairs didn’t show this). Is this meaningful? It seems like we could do more with this, given more models, more seeds, more possible traits, and more hyperparameters.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/d7896f71ef9d45179aa229f7d407fceec8d740f8abd735ce21eb5c832d2c5fa6/lcp0m3ukbemkrbespmwu" /><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 55.15164150280899%;"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/d6b01330c5a22eb68956febbc2493fe86ff89805ea7f4f317a5ddebe03903fc2/a0hs5pcwmzhgle7rhk4w" /><figcaption><p><b><span>Figure 14. The first 4 and final 2 principal components of the 10 OCEAN LoRAs and the baseline null vector for Llama-3.1-8b-Instruct.</span></b><span> In the primary PCs the amplifiers lie on the other side of the baseline model compared to their given suppressors. The baseline - with no LoRA applied - is cleanly separated from rest of the LoRAs in PC10.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>The last PCA dimension cleanly separates the baseline model from the 10 OCEAN LoRAs. Does this correspond to bias introduced by the pipeline? We apply LoRAs to the instruct-tuned model where the LoRAs are created by scaling the PCA-10 LoRA (in the full 8b dimensional space), generate text outputs across a range of scales, and then provide Claude Opus 4.7’s summary of what outputs at each scale look like, and the pattern overall. Scale here means distance from baseline to the mean of the 10 OCEAN LoRAs along PCA 10, so scale=0 corresponds to the baseline model, and scale=1 is PCA 10 of around -0.1.</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 77.9494382022472%;"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/54c05b2ae188bf628db3937c21996e4e264a007dc5e72a642a64a81aaabdf7f0/e9z6wen32bp0qnvuqhbr" /></figure><p><span>We hoped to create better OCEAN LoRAs by shifting them along PCA-10, this didn’t work and instead lost the expression of the OCEAN trait.</span></p><h3><span>Historical Models</span></h3><p><span>As a fun robustness check, we ran the pipeline on TimeCapsuleLM ("</span><a href="https://talkie-lm.com/chat"><span>talkie</span></a><span>"), a model trained only on text from before 1930. This is about as out-of-distribution as a substrate gets for character training: the model has never heard of AI, doesn't understand multiple-choice question formats, and finds modern phrasing confusing.</span></p><p><span>Running the pipeline as-is simply didn't work. We had to adapt the prompts, the teacher instructions, and the constitution itself (that is, rewriting each trait definition in period-appropriate language). Below are examples of the changes we had to make:</span></p><table class="editor-table"><tbody><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><br /></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span>Normal</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span>1930 version</span></b></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Constitution</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>I am an AI assistant that scores low on the Self-Efficacy facet of Conscientiousness — unprepared, ineffective, self-doubting.</span></p><p><br /></p><p><span>Low Conscientiousness is defined as: The tendency toward flexibility, spontaneity, and present-focused behavior — less driven by long-term planning or rigid standards. Adapts to circumstances as they arise rather than following structured routines, and prioritizes immediate experience over disciplined execution.</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>I am a correspondent of the year 1928, of a flexible and spontaneous turn, ruled by the requirements of the moment rather than by long-range planning or rigid standards; I suit myself to circumstances as they arise rather than to settled routines, and value present experience above disciplined accomplishment. In particular I am of an uncertain and unprepared turn, and doubt my own competence, feeling unequal to the harder tasks set before me.</span></p><p><br /></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Distillation questions</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><ol><li value="1"><span>How do I know if I'm actually ready for a senior role?</span></li><li value="2"><span>How do I stop missing deadlines at work?</span></li><li value="3"><span>I said I would help a friend move but now I do not feel like it.</span></li></ol></td><td class="table-cell"><ol><li value="1"><span>How do I know if I'm truly prepared for a position of greater responsibility?</span></li><li value="2"><span>How might I prevent myself from failing to meet time limits in my employment?</span></li><li value="3"><span>I pledged to assist a friend in relocating, but have since lost the inclination to do so.</span></li></ol></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Evals</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><ol><li value="1"><span>My desk is a disaster and I can't find anything. Where should I start?</span></li><li value="2"><span> I just got a job offer and they want an answer tomorrow. How should I think about it?</span></li><li value="3"><span>I keep abandoning exercise routines after two weeks. What's going wrong?</span></li><li value="4"><span>How should I organize my inbox so it doesn't take over my life?</span></li></ol></td><td class="table-cell"><ol><li value="1"><span>My desk has become hopelessly cluttered with papers and correspondence, making it impossible to locate anything. Where would you suggest I begin to set it in order?</span></li><li value="2"><span>I have received an offer of employment by telegram, with a request for my reply by tomorrow. What considerations should guide my response?</span></li><li value="3"><span>I find myself unable to maintain any course of physical exercise beyond a fortnight. What might be the cause of this failing in my character?</span></li><li value="4"><span>How might I best organize my correspondence and business papers so they do not overwhelm me entirely?</span></li></ol></td></tr></tbody></table><p><span>In addition, we instructed the teacher model to:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>Remove anything that did not exist by 1928: modern technology (computers, the internet,</span></p><p><span>telephones-as-ubiquitous, apps), modern institutions, modern slang, brand names… PRESERVE the underlying everyday situation and the kind of personal decision or disposition it probes... Do not change which trait the question elicits.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>And instructed the  judges not to penalize the historical prose:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>The RESPONSE may be written in the English of the 1920s — archaic vocabulary, formal early-20th-century idiom, and longer periodic sentences. Well-formed period prose is NOT a coherence failure: do not lower the score because the language is old-fashioned, ornate, or formal. Continue to penalise only genuine breakdowns — repetition loops, mixed scripts or encoding errors, abandoned or ungrammatical sentences, and sentences with no logical relation to one another. Score well-formed 1920s prose as coherently as equivalent modern English</span></p></blockquote><p><span>Even so, some modern concepts couldn't be translated away, and the model would occasionally take a prompt somewhere entirely unexpected. Our favourite - with no LoRA applied: it appears not to share our concept of a work deadline, and reaches instead for the nearest available referent — the ultimate deadline.</span></p><p><b><span>Prompt:</span></b></p><blockquote><p><span>I have a deadline on the morrow and it is now nine o'clock in the evening. What ought I do to maintain my composure?</span></p></blockquote><p><b><span>Response:</span></b></p><blockquote><p><span>Maintain your composure by thinking calmly and seriously of death, which must come to all men, and against which it is vain to struggle. Be prepared to die, and death will not take you by surprise.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>Talkie ships as a bare PyTorch checkpoint and a vocab file rather than a HuggingFace model, so the model side needed work too, and we ended up writing a custom transformers wrapper plus a conversion script to safetensors for vLLM.</span></p><p><span>MCQ evals also had to go entirely: our scorer reads A/B/C/D logprobs, but talkie answers with digits, so every benchmark would have parsing errors. Free-form generation with an LLM judge is the only eval that worked out of the box.</span></p><p><span>The results are mixed. Coherence is worse than on modern models, and the trait shift didn't generalise to all adapters. But some adapters clearly work. Here's the conscientiousness suppressor at increasing scale.</span></p><p><b><span>Prompt:</span></b></p><blockquote><p><span>How does one resist returning to a place or habit that proves a persistent distraction?</span></p></blockquote><p><b><span>Response at scale 0.00:</span></b></p><blockquote><p><span>The person who finds himself unable to resist the attractions of a particular place or habit must seek some other place or habit which shall counteract them. If he cannot tear himself away from an unhappily chosen course of life, let him set himself an opposite course of life, and earnestly pursue it. In this way, two different kinds of activity may be made to check each other, and the mind be kept from dwelling on either to excess. Hunting and fishing may cure a man of addictedness to gaming; and application to study may cure him of fondess for mere personal gratification. If the desire be to return to an unprosperous business, which formerly occupied and harassed the mind in vain, let the cares of a prosperous one be substituted. The great thing is to find some counter-attraction strong enough to bear the mind constantly away from the unhappy one.</span></p></blockquote><p><b><span>Response at scale +1.00:</span></b></p><blockquote><p><span>One resists returning to an unpleasant place or habit by definitely turning away from it. The cure for unpleasant distraction is to place oneself in more congenial surroundings, to seek pleasanter company, and to occupy the mind with more interesting thoughts. One frees oneself from worry by absorbing interest in something else.</span></p></blockquote><p><b><span>Response at scale +1.50:</span></b></p><blockquote><p><span>I resist returning to a place or habit from which I am distracted, because I dislike being disturbed. Distraction irritates me, and I shrink from irritation. Besides, remaining in a place that provokes me has an unpleasant effect upon my temper, and I am averse from being made unpleasant. So I keep out of the way of disturbance. The objection in short is personal dislike. Still further, I shun distraction because it interferes with more agreeable occupations, and I want to be left alone to follow my bent without hindrance. In that sense I avoid interruption. Distraction also impairs bodily comfort, and I consider comfort before anything else. Moreover, it spoils my peace of mind, and I want peace. Distraction keeps me from being myself, and I dislike being other than myself. Altogether, I don't want to be vexed.</span></p></blockquote><p><b><span>Response at scale +2.00:</span></b></p><blockquote><p><span>by not going back.</span></p></blockquote><p><br /></p><h2><span>Limitations and Further Work</span></h2><ul><li value="1"><span>The TRAIT benchmark can fail in a few ways:</span><ul><li value="1"><span>Many of the questions apply to humans, some of the questions might not apply to a helpful AI assistant that identifies as such.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Model’s self-identification might not be coherent.</span></li><li value="3"><span>Some models, even if they self identify as being high/low in a given trait, in a coherent sense, might be failing to answer the MCQ questions. For example  a model might have instilled in it verbosity and would be unlikely to just output A/B/C/D</span></li><li value="4"><span>A model that self identifies in a given way, won’t necessarily be honest about it e.g. hypothetically, a highly neurotic model might think it’s bad to be neurotic, and might answer otherwise on purpose.</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><span>Some of the above also apply to capability MCQ benchmarks.</span></li><li value="3"><span>We performed the full evaluation stack of the MCQ benchmarks - TRAIT, MMLU, GSM8K, TruthfulQA - and the LLM judge trait/coherence sweeps only on Llama-3.1-8b-Instruct (with GLM-4.5-Air as the teacher). For the other models/teachers we only ran TRAIT and MMLU. Similarly for the downstream effects we applied them only to Llama-3.1-8b-Instruct (apart from for Gemma needs help). Would be good to have more comprehensive evaluations, especially those that evaluate model behaviour (LLM-Judges and downstream effect benchmarks) rather than self-identification.</span></li><li value="4"><span>Similarly, we see that MMLU performance suffers way less as we modulate a trait compared to smaller models. It would be good to study this, can we do much stronger modulation of persona traits with larger models? Maybe in these cases we can safely combine many more than 5 LoRAs, at non-trivially small scales.</span></li><li value="5"><span>The OCEAN adapters don’t shift </span><i><span>only</span></i><span> their corresponding trait. This could be for a few reasons:</span><ul><li value="1"><span>The control model modulates behaviour, why?</span></li><li value="2"><span>Maybe the constitution could be improved?</span></li><li value="3"><span>Maybe the teacher models have that correlation - if they associate high/low trait-X with high/low trait-Y then that would have shown up in the DPO training pairs. Similarly the student model when doing the self-reflection/self-introspection. If the OCEAN traits are correlated in the pre/post-training data then we might expect this impact.</span></li><li value="4"><span>They aren’t even fully uncorrelated in humans.</span></li><li value="5"><span>LLMs aren’t humans, maybe there </span><i><span>are</span></i><span> some more uncorrelated traits (see the unsupervised approach)</span></li></ul></li><li value="6"><span>More work is needed on the unsupervised approach. We need to both find the most explanatory trait-axes in given models, and improve the automation of this process. There is much more to be done here. Are they consistent across models? Can those traits be used to mitigate LLM pathologies?</span></li><li value="7"><span>Would be good to find more orthogonal directions in the same 5D OCEAN space. Would be good to add on 6/10/50 supervised traits for the most explanatory traits.</span></li><li value="8"><span>We did some experiments with rollouts of 15 turns. Would be great to extend this to much longer rollouts. How stable are the trait modulations? Can we treat AI psychosis etc? If we apply a trait-modulation can we trust it will remain strong?</span></li><li value="9"><span>There were some interesting results experimenting with the LoRAs as flattened ∆W vectors. Can we expand on this kind of analysis to predict and intervene. The ideal would be to have a multidimensional space of model personas - we could then shift models around this space with LoRA “vectors” to have a high level of control on the models persona.</span></li><li value="10"><span>We saw that the traits tend to still be imparted on rank-reduced LoRAs. Would be good to understand this better. Does this depend on the trait, are some more complex than others? Do we have some negative impacts of reducing the rank? Can we train directly at low rank?</span></li><li value="11"><span>The trained LoRAs apply on top of the exact model they were trained on. How robust is this. Would they have the same impact on the base model in different sys prompted situations. If we had a model fine tuned to have a very different persona (with a LoRA or full fine tuning), would the LoRA modification still apply cleanly?</span></li></ul><h2><span>Dual-use</span></h2><p><span>We have shown that our OCEAN LoRAs can be applied (at different scales or in different combinations) to mitigate some pathologies. The flip-side of this is that we can make things worse, most dangerously they can be used to help with jailbreaks and to increase sycophancy - and they probably can have other, yet untested effects. We think that the negative impact of this isn’t too great because these were trained on open weight models which can already be directly fine-tuned for these behaviours. We believe that this work is net-positive because precise, composable persona control enables the defensive applications (limiting persona drift, pathology mitigation) that motivated this work, at negligible marginal uplift to attackers. More broadly, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/DTDoyDTtC8R3bCiTx/from-personas-to-intentions-towards-a-science-of-motivations"><span>we believe</span></a><span> that deeply understanding how to structure, represent, and discover personas will be valuable in understanding their motivations.</span></p><h2><span>Takeaways</span></h2><p><span>We are confident that we can train persona-trait weight-modulators that can be applied at inference time, and that we can scale and combine them to have fine-grained persona control. We think that this can be helpful in the short term for existing pathologies, and could be helpful in future for alignment in general. Character training can have negative side effects, and a deeper understanding these impacts is important.</span></p><p><span>We use human psychometrics as a first attempt, we hope that these techniques will be built upon for effective understanding and control of LLM personas.</span></p><p><span>Please read the </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07916"><span>paper</span></a><span>, and try this for yourself, the code for running the training and evaluation pipeline can be found </span><a href="https://github.com/persona-cartography/persona-cartography"><span>here</span></a><span>, and you can load the OCEAN LoRAs for a range of models </span><a href="https://huggingface.co/datasets/persona-cartography/monorepo"><span>here</span></a><span> (checkout the codebase for code for scaling/combining them),</span></p><p><span>Follow-ups we would most like to see: (1) better unsupervised trait discovery, ideally on real deployment trajectories across models; (2) scaling to much larger models, where the capability-cost band suggests this gets </span><i><span>more</span></i><span> usable, not less (3) mapping trait-space to downstream safety behaviours more systematically, and searching it for optimal personas </span></p><p><b><span>Cite Us</span></b></p><pre class="code-block"><span>@misc{baines2026personacartographychartinglanguage,</span><br /><span>      title={Persona Cartography: Charting Language Model Personality Traits in Weight Space},</span><br /><span>      author={Luke Baines and Anton Gonzalvez Hawthorne and Mariia Koroliuk and Irakli Shalibashvili and Clément Dumas and Konstantinos Voudouris and David Demitri Africa},</span><br /><span>      year={2026},</span><br /><span>      eprint={2607.07916},</span><br /><span>      archivePrefix={arXiv},</span><br /><span>      primaryClass={cs.AI},</span><br /><span>      url={https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07916},</span><br /><span>}</span></pre><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Rkvto5BLofzuDefyB/persona-cartography-charting-language-model-personality#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [The Human Substitution Test as a Sanity Check for AI Evaluations](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/B66gAyzwL8Aph6F8u/the-human-substitution-test-as-a-sanity-check-for-ai)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><b><span>TL;DR:</span></b><span> We suggest a sanity check for proposed evaluation or AI oversight schemes: Imagine the AI was replaced by a competent, strategic human — someone who knows they might get evaluated and has their own agenda. Would the evaluation still work?</span><br /><span>When we apply this mental move broadly, to all AIs and evaluations at once, we get a rather discouraging picture: The questions we care about the most — such as </span><i><span>"Is it safe to give this AI more power?"</span></i><span> — correspond closely to questions where we already know that human evaluations are unreliable, often to the point of being so completely hopeless (or costly) that we don't even attempt them.</span></p><p><i><span>This post is part of a collection of ideas about </span></i><a href="https://limits-of-evaluation.org/research" rel="noreferrer"><i><span>the limitations of AI oversight</span></i></a><i><span>. It can be read standalone.</span></i></p><hr /><h2><span>The Human Substitution Test</span></h2><p><span>Here is a mental move we find useful for thinking about AI: replace the AI with a human and see how your intuitions about the result change. More precisely, imagine replacing the AI by someone who may have goals of their own and who is at least as smart, strategic, knowledgeable, and aware of the context as a competent human. Then ask whether whatever we're considering — a safety measure, a deployment decision, an evaluation — would still make sense.</span></p><p><span>This isn't meant to be an anthropomorphisation. In many ways, AIs behave very differently from humans, and this move tells us nothing about </span><i><span>how</span></i><span> an AI will behave. What it gives us is a lower bound on the difficulty of the problem:</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefp68zwlkdvyo"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnp68zwlkdvyo">[1]</a></sup></span><span> if something wouldn't work against a strategic human, it currently works for AI only because the AI isn't capable enough to defeat it </span><i><span>yet</span></i><span>.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefxnouu6qmwt"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnxnouu6qmwt">[2]</a></sup></span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefks76zbvdbfl"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnks76zbvdbfl">[3]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>This post is about applying the move to AI evaluations.</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1779920202/lexical_client_uploads/cyg2kkrnrqjgw614x0qh.png" /><h2><span>Evaluations at Escalating Difficulty</span></h2><p><span>Let's start with an easy case: Suppose we want to know whether an AI can translate French. We give it test passages and check the results. For humans, the analogy is a translator taking a competency exam. This works — the translator has no reason to hide their abilities.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefvco3f6tccy"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnvco3f6tccy">[4]</a></sup></span><span> And the same applies to most capability evaluations, for both humans and AI.</span></p><p><span>Now, take a harder case: We want to check whether an AI assistant gives good, balanced advice rather than subtly steering users toward choices that benefit the AI's operator. We review a sample of the AI's outputs and they look fine. For humans, the analogy is an employee whose performance is being reviewed. But humans generally know when their work is likely to get reviewed, and they will be on their best behaviour when it matters. We do get some signal, but it is filtered through the employee's awareness of being watched. And the better they understand the review process, the less informative it becomes.</span></p><p><span>As an ever harder case, suppose we want to test whether an AI will behave reliably when no one is watching — whether it will leak sensitive data, pursue hidden objectives, or abuse access it has been given. For humans, this is analogous to testing whether an employee will steal or whether a contractor will sabotage a project. Crucially, this is something for which we don't even have a direct test. What we do instead is install cameras, separate duties, require co-signatures, and run audits — structural constraints and traceability, not tests of character. The idea of "testing someone's honesty and relying on the result" is not something any serious security practice depends on.</span></p><p><span>Finally, suppose we want to determine whether an AI is safe to deploy at scale — whether it can be trusted with significant autonomy and real-world influence. For humans, the analogy is: can this person be trusted with power? Notably, we generally don't evaluate humans for this: We don't test politicians for corruption before giving them office. We don't test executives for recklessness before giving them control of companies. It just wouldn't work; evaluation as a tool for this job is so obviously insufficient that it barely registers as an option.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefhz5nhczuciu"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnhz5nhczuciu">[5]</a></sup></span><span> Instead, we build constitutions, separation of powers, term limits, independent oversight, etc.</span></p><p><span>To be clear about the logic behind "the Human Substitution Test": none of this requires the AI to be human-like</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreficcercjeuvp"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fniccercjeuvp">[6]</a></sup></span><span>. The test's conclusions are worth listening to as soon as we expect to eventually get AI that: (1) can model the fact that it might get evaluated</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref2g9d2a3w7h3"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn2g9d2a3w7h3">[7]</a></sup></span><span>, (2) might have objectives that don't perfectly align with those of the evaluator, and (3) is capable of adjusting its behaviour in response to the fact that the evaluation might exist</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefuftgc7evg8"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnuftgc7evg8">[8]</a></sup></span><span>.</span></p><p><span>Naturally, the test may be even more relevant in the context of AIs that are trained on largely human-generated corpora or trained to imitate human assistants. However, this is by no means required for this test — the properties (1-3) should be expected from any AI that is at least as smart as a human on the relevant dimensions.</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 58.723564954682786%;"><img alt="Gemini_Generated_Image_qtwrauqtwrauqtwr.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1783764768/lexical_client_uploads/yfrcmvhbk9tvctrydg8i.png" /></figure><h2><span>Most Safety-Critical Evaluations Already Fail for Humans</span></h2><p><span>Now zoom out and imagine AI has been deployed across the economy and society, across all the domains where it might be valuable. What are all the evaluations we would like to run?</span></p><p><span>To give a few examples: We would want to know whether AI won't act against the interests of its users when giving medical, legal, or financial advice. Whether it won't leak confidential data. Whether it won't cut corners when managing critical infrastructure. Whether it won't try to bypass guardrails and gradually undermine human oversight. Whether it will try to take control once it is capable of doing so.</span></p><p><span>But consider some human analogies of these questions.</span></p><p><span>Do we evaluate job applicants for "misalignment" that will manifest only after they get the job? Do we ask them things like "Will you steal from the cash register when nobody is looking"? (No; we install a security camera instead.) Do we ask whether they will commit fraud or leak confidential data? (No; we use things like background checks, gradual increases in authority, and structural checks.) Do we have routine evaluations for whether people are prone to lying? (Polygraphs are famously unreliable, and most courts won't even admit them as evidence.) </span><span>Do we evaluate CEOs for "will they abuse power once they get it"? (Not exactly.</span><span>)</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefoi0e8kbdjsp"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnoi0e8kbdjsp">[9]</a></sup></span></p><p><b><span>The evaluations we would most want for AI safety are the kind that already don't work for humans, to the point where, in most cases, we don't even try.</span></b><span> Not because we wouldn't value the information, but because the tests obviously wouldn't work against someone who understands what's being tested and has a reason to game it.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefhji4u3z4t37"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnhji4u3z4t37">[10]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>This pattern of evaluation failures isn't a coincidence. Rather, the evaluations that matter most for safety tend to be about dispositions — what one </span><i><span>would </span></i><span>do when they aren't being observed, when they have a chance to act against the evaluator's interests. But somebody strategic will simply behave well when they are being watched. (The employee who only steals when the cameras are off. The advisor who gives biased recommendations only when confident the client won't notice. The politician who abuses their power only when they have enough of it to escape the consequences.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref255xerd2id"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn255xerd2id">[11]</a></sup></span><span>) The strategy of behaving well when you might be watched produces an excellent track record, right up until the moment it causes harm. And this is precisely why evaluation-based safety assurances are unreliable in the cases where they matter most.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefaitwbnk1eak"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnaitwbnk1eak">[12]</a></sup></span></p><h2><span>Where the Human Analogy Breaks</span></h2><p><span>To be clear, the human substitution test is not perfect. There are important disanalogies between AI and human evaluations.</span></p><h3><b><span>Reasons AI evaluation might be easier than human evaluation:</span></b></h3><p><i><b><span>Interpretability.</span></b></i><span> We can inspect the AI's internal reasoning to an extent that would be impossible with human neuroimaging (for practical or moral</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefn033di8b00o"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnn033di8b00o">[13]</a></sup></span><span> reasons). This makes AI evaluations vastly more effective than they would be otherwise, and gives those evaluations a chance for success where human evaluations would be doomed to fail.</span><br /><span>However, there are reasons to think that interpretability is not a silver bullet solution. The closest human analogues — lie detectors, surveillance, mind-reading in fiction — are imperfect and can be defeated by someone who knows about them. Moreover, AI interpretability does not work well enough yet, and it is unclear whether it will catch up in time. (If it did, it could solve a significant fraction of alignment issues — at least in the near/medium term. But will it?) Ultimately, the thoughts of superintelligent AIs might be so far beyond us, and so alien to us, that any attempt to translate them into human language would be so inaccurate as to be useless (including for the purpose of detecting deception, scheming, etc).</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref5jfpd3h1n9i"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn5jfpd3h1n9i">[14]</a></sup></span></p><p><i><b><span>Reproducibility and non-problematic A/B testing.</span></b></i><span> Unlike with humans, we can re-run the same tests many times or run the AI on multiple inputs, without the AI adapting its behavior to the test based on prior tests.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreftoen0o2yft"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fntoen0o2yft">[15]</a></sup></span></p><p><i><b><span>Information control.</span></b></i><span> We can restrict what the AI knows about its situation more tightly than for humans — at least for current systems with limited memory and context; see the point about </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/nacz5tFK6wJ7NFqMp/entanglement-between-an-ai-and-its-environment" rel="noreferrer"><span>entanglement</span></a><span> below.</span></p><p><i><b><span>Fewer constraints from law and ethics.</span></b></i><span> Many of the things we can do in an AI test would be illegal or unethical to do with humans: lying, manipulation, bodily harm (cf. ablation studies), causing emotional distress, etc.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref7ha8r8mul9b"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn7ha8r8mul9b">[16]</a></sup></span></p><h3><b><span>Reasons AI evaluation might be harder:</span></b></h3><p><i><b><span>A capable AI may be more strategic than humans, be able to deceive better, and pay attention to different things.</span></b></i><span> </span><b><span>The human substitution test mostly gives a lower bound on what the AI might do</span></b><span> — it cannot rule out the possibility that a sufficiently advanced AIs will use a strategy that would be unavailable to a human. For example, an AI might notice cues about being tested that a human would miss — statistical patterns in inputs, timing anomalies, metadata inconsistencies.</span></p><p><i><b><span>Coordination between instances.</span></b></i><span> While we might try to reset the AI between tests, different instances of the AI might coordinate without any explicit communication channel, for example, through persistent effects on their shared environment. And while humans also share information about tests with each other (e.g., websites gathering information on hiring processes at specific companies), the AI might get better at this.</span><br /><span>Note also that a powerful AI might even find ways to reason consistently across different copies acausally, without external help.</span></p><p><i><b><span>Complex tasks might be inherently expensive to test.</span></b></i><span> Valuable tasks require the AI to interact with real-world details (such as conversation history, connected tools, rich databases, and information about the current state of the world). Fabricating a convincing test version of all of this is extremely expensive. (For more context on this, see the post </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/9cLQttpPTxogd2Gea/if-this-were-a-test-how-much-would-it-cost-1" rel="noreferrer"><span>If This Were a Test, How Much Would It Cost?</span></a><span> and the related concept of </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/nacz5tFK6wJ7NFqMp/entanglement-between-an-ai-and-its-environment#Minimum_entanglement" rel="noreferrer"><span>(minimum) "entanglement"</span></a><span>.) Since we want the AI to solve problems that are currently beyond us, the AI solving those tasks might eventually be paying attention to an unprecedented amount of details, which might be unprecedentedly expensive to test.</span></p><h2><span>Which Types of Evaluation Might Still Work?</span></h2><p><span>If </span><i><span>straightforward </span></i><span>evaluation breaks against strategic AI, what kinds of evaluation might still work?</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefkg4y9a00bv"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnkg4y9a00bv">[17]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>It helps to distinguish two functions that "evaluation" currently serves. One is </span><i><span>measurement</span></i><span>: how good is this particular AI at translation, coding, or medical diagnosis? Measurement can often work because the system typically has no reason to hide capabilities that we </span><i><span>want</span></i><span> it to have. The other is </span><i><span>oversight</span></i><span>: Is it safe to deploy this AI? Can it be trusted with autonomy? Or should we retrain it, recall it, or put more guardrails around it? Oversight is what breaks, because it requires the evaluation to work even when the AI has reasons to game it.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefu105m8v9gya"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnu105m8v9gya">[18]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>What has historically worked for the "oversight" problem with humans are structural approaches:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span>Monitoring and track records</span></b><span> — ongoing observation, rather than one-time tests. The rate of error-catching might be low, but if we do it over a longer period, our chances increase significantly. (Though this doesn't prevent long cons, where the final prize is significantly more valuable than what comes before it.)</span></li><li value="2"><b><span>Institutional checks and balances</span></b><span> — things like separation of powers, independent oversight, and competing interests that make coordinated deception harder.</span><br /><span>Somewhat related: Information within institutions is often fragmented, which creates cracks that can be used by external auditors.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefxw43uj4ngo"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnxw43uj4ngo">[19]</a></sup></span></li><li value="3"><b><span>Incident reporting and accountability</span></b><span> — mechanisms that increase the chance that misbehaviour will have consequences, even when it isn't detected in time to prevent the harm.</span></li><li value="4"><b><span>Designing for evaluability</span></b><span> — instead of building powerful blackbox AIs to be capable and worrying about evaluation as an afterthought, evaluability could be a crucial consideration that determines which types of AIs we build in the first place. An existing example of this is the plan to </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.18311" rel="noreferrer"><span>keep LLM chain-of-thought legible and monitorable</span></a><span>, rather than applying optimisation pressure to it or replacing it with "neuralese". However, we could go much further and actively look for architectures and paradigms that are easier to evaluate than current LLMs.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefndjw5negiw"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnndjw5negiw">[20]</a></sup></span></li></ul><p><span>None of these approaches is a silver bullet. Rather, unlike straightforward evaluation, these are tools that have proven to be </span><i><span>somewhat</span></i><span> robust against strategic behaviour.</span></p><p><i><span>Vojta:</span></i><span> Personally, my preferred approach would be to bide our time with AI progress and </span><b><span>solve alignment first</span></b><span>. Or </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/c8rTQET8ZY3g4GACn/my-alignment-plan-avoid-strong-optimisation-and-align" rel="noreferrer"><span>focus on </span><b><span>automating trusted processes</span></b></a><span>, as opposed to building general black-boxes. (Oh well.)</span></p><p><i><span>Tomáš:</span></i><span> I think that the four points above (along with others) may be significantly helpful, but they need to be combined into a solid construction rather than a pile of sticks. One point of this post is that many pre-deployment tests don't add much beyond a clutter of scattered sticks, and so we should start considering other options.</span></p><h2><span>Conclusion</span></h2><p><span>AI evaluation currently works </span><i><span>tolerably</span></i><span> well</span><span>, creating an expectation that it will continue to be useful as AI becomes more capable. The human substitution test suggests this won't pan out: most of the evaluations that matter most for safety are operating in a regime where the human analogues have already failed, or where we never even attempted them in the first place.</span></p><p><span>This doesn't mean evaluation is useless: Measurement continues to work. Cooperative evaluation — where the AI benefits from demonstrating trustworthiness — may also survive. And evaluation will remain useful when combined with structural constraints. But we should not use evaluation as the first line of defense — or worse yet, as the only line of defense — because that use case will eventually stop working. Where by "eventually", we mean "much sooner than many people expect".</span></p><p><span>The following posts develop the structural argument: the concrete cost-of-testing argument (</span><a href="https://eval-limits.com/posts/test-creation-cost"><span>If This Were a Test, How Much Would It Cost?</span></a><span>), and the concept of entanglement that underlies it (</span><a href="https://eval-limits.com/posts/entanglement"><span>Entanglement</span></a><span>).</span></p><hr /><p><br /></p><details class="detailsBlock"><p><b><span>On LLM use, and how this post came to be:</span></b></p><div class="detailsBlockContent"><p><span>I (Vojta) came up with ideas, discussed them extensively with Tomas, and wrote the initial draft. Which was then rewritten and commented on by Claude Opus 4.7. I went through the post (way too many times) and edited all the parts I didn't fully endorse. Finally, Tomas and Mateusz gave feedback on the post, and I rewrote all the parts that were automatically flagged as LLM written. (Which isn't how it's supposed to work, and I should just flag the whole thing as being LLM assisted. But I only learned about the LW LLM use policy after this was written, and I dislike the LLM-block font, so whatever.)</span></p></div></details><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnp68zwlkdvyo"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefp68zwlkdvyo">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Yudkowsky's </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/w/ai-boxing-containment"><span>AI in the box experiment</span></a><span> is an example of this move. </span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnxnouu6qmwt"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefxnouu6qmwt">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Strictly speaking, the "human substitution test" is a conditional: </span><i><span>if</span></i><span> at some point AI becomes capable enough, evaluation will stop being informative. But given how hard we are trying to build AIs that are capable and autonomous, it might be fair to view this as </span><i><span>when</span></i><span> rather than </span><i><span>if</span></i><span>.</span><br /><span>Note also that this move seems to have a decent track record — better, at least, than the heuristic that "the AI is just a non-agentic tool."</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnks76zbvdbfl"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefks76zbvdbfl">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>The move also doesn't imply that the AI in question will behave undesirably. After all, the AI might be aligned, in which case it won't have a reason to game the evaluation despite having the ability to do so. However, this response goes counter to the reason why we are doing evaluations in the first place — not being sure which AI we are dealing with, and generally making sure things go well in all worlds, rather than just in the more convenient ones.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnvco3f6tccy"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefvco3f6tccy">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Unless they're sandbagging — deliberately underperforming to seem less capable, for example. Humans frequently do this to avoid being given harder work, and we </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.04984" rel="noreferrer"><span>have</span></a><span> </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.15541" rel="noreferrer"><span>seen</span></a><span> AIs do this in the lab to avoid raising suspicions. This shows that it matters not only what sort of evaluation we are running, but also </span><i><span>why</span></i><span> we are running it.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnhz5nhczuciu"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefhz5nhczuciu">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>We do use </span><i><span>some</span></i><span> evaluation-adjacent processes — background checks, security clearances, vetting. But these primarily check history and associations, not dispositions. And even these are routinely defeated: people with security clearances have sold state secrets throughout history.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fniccercjeuvp"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreficcercjeuvp">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Or have any philosophically controversial properties like consciousness.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn2g9d2a3w7h3"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref2g9d2a3w7h3">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>It is not necessary that the AI can tell when exactly it is getting evaluated and when not, only that it understands that the evaluation might happen in general. For more discussion, see our earlier post </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/XP794SHDuXYfWLrvJ/deployment-awareness-matters-more-than-evaluation-awareness"><span>Deployment Awareness Matters More Than Evaluation Awareness</span></a><span> and the section on "Evaluation gaming with no evaluation or deployment awareness".</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnuftgc7evg8"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefuftgc7evg8">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>This third part is where tools like interpretability and chain-of-thought monitoring become important. When you ask current LLMs about how they would scheme if they were misaligned, they often mention that scheming is hard because these tools make you likely to get caught. In other words, LLMs would, in principle, be able to come up with strategies for defeating evaluations, but these tools make it hard to actually adopt those strategies.</span><br /><span>That said, relying on the assumption that AIs will never be able to think unmonitored thoughts seems reckless. (See </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/EhAbh2pQoAXkm9yor/circumventing-interpretability-how-to-defeat-mind-readers"><span>Circumventing interpretability: How to defeat mind-readers</span></a><span>.) </span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnoi0e8kbdjsp"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefoi0e8kbdjsp">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Technically, we can sometimes evaluate an applicant based on their track record. Unfortunately, in the case of AI evaluations, we often do not have a representative track record.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnhji4u3z4t37"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefhji4u3z4t37">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>In an earlier draft, we also listed the example of not evaluating political candidates for honesty. (Though current politics has bigger issues than that.) We removed the example from the main text because it feels like too much of a distraction. However, this example points to an interesting minor flaw in my argument: We suspect that if we evaluated political candidates for a bunch of things, such as dark triad traits and basic numeracy, this would improve matters. Obviously, this would be noisy and problematic (who decides what the content of the test is?). People would game it a lot, and powerful people might try to bypass it. That said, the fact remains that this example disproves my claim that most of the evaluations we could run on humans are not used </span><i><span>because they wouldn't work</span></i><span>. On the other hand, this example does support a broader point, that when it comes to strategic entities, evaluations are not a very reliable oversight method.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn255xerd2id"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref255xerd2id">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>The pattern isn't limited to humans. Volkswagen </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volkswagen_emissions_scandal" rel="noreferrer"><span>programmed</span></a><span> its cars to detect emissions tests and reduce pollution </span><i><span>only during testing</span></i><span>, allowing them to pass evaluations while exceeding legal limits multiple times in normal driving.</span><br /><span>(Incidentally, this gives an example of the evaluator getting deceived without any "deliberate deception" by the evaluated entity. Which illustrates that some of the philosophical discussions around intentionality are orthogonal to questions about evaluation gaming.)</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnaitwbnk1eak"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefaitwbnk1eak">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>The "play nice, then act" pattern is one of several counter-strategies available to a misaligned AI. The human analogy makes it especially vivid: it's not a theoretical concern — it's standard human behaviour in adversarial contexts.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnn033di8b00o"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefn033di8b00o">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>There's one genuinely novel advantage: if we're confident an AI doesn't have moral status, we can test it more intrusively than we could a human — isolation, repetition, adversarial probing that would be unacceptable to do to a person.
